#5160. A Brazilian crypto exchange plans to show customers their money, not let them move it

Mercado Bitcoin plans view-only access after its security incident. Crypto balances reflect November 2, while withdrawals and trading await later stages.

Brazilian crypto exchange Mercado Bitcoin plans to restore account viewing gradually on Nov. 9, allowing customers to inspect their holdings while withdrawals and trading remain unavailable after its Nov. 2 security incident.

The exchange's recovery plan says customers will be able to see balances by asset and category on its website. Its app will offer a summary of holdings, with API access returning later. Deposits and transfers also remain unavailable, along with cards, payment services including Pix and TED, new products and account opening.

When CryptoSlate reported the shutdown on Nov. 2, the exchange had provided no reopening timetable. Mercado Bitcoin set out the Nov. 9 viewing stage in its Nov. 4 announcement, saying transaction services would follow as security checks were completed.

Customers should expect a historical view of their crypto positions. According to the exchange, the quantities shown will reflect their accounts on Nov. 2 before the incident. Their valuations in Brazilian reais will also use prices from that moment, with prices updated when trading resumes.

That means seeing a crypto balance will give customers a reference for their recorded holdings, without enabling them to sell or withdraw those assets. The displayed BRL valuation will not yet show how subsequent market price moves have changed their value.

Mercado Bitcoin plans view-only access on Nov. 9. Its first stage shows recorded holdings while transactions stay on hold.

Planned viewing would show the Nov. 2 crypto snapshot while transaction services await later stages.

Company timetable; later stages have no dates. The snapshot applies to crypto quantities and their BRL valuations, not all investment categories.

Schematic information path and service sequence from Mercado Bitcoin's recovery guidance. Solid arrows show the displayed snapshot feeding viewing access; a closed gate marks unavailable transactions. Stage spacing has no time or quantitative scale.

Company timetable; later stages have no dates. The snapshot applies to crypto quantities and their BRL valuations, not all investment categories.

Orders placed before the shutdown will need attention too. Mercado Bitcoin says it canceled limit orders that were open when the platform went offline on Nov. 2. Customers will be able to submit fresh orders once trading returns.

The interruption also affected yield. The exchange says it temporarily discontinued its Ethereum staking product for security reasons, so ETH in that product earned no return during the interruption. It says other staking and digital-dollar passive-income products continued accruing, with accumulated passive-income returns to be credited when those products reactivate.

The FAQ puts Pix and digital fixed-income payments in the next recovery stage, with trading and cryptoasset transfers following later. It provides no dates for those subsequent steps, leaving customers without a timetable for moving their crypto.

Before balance movements resume in those stages, all customers will have to reconfirm registration details and set new passwords through the app, the company says.

Mercado Bitcoin says customer balance records remain preserved and that it is replenishing affected assets with its own funds.

The exchange also warns customers to type its official website address directly into their browser. It says it does not send links to release balances or ask for passwords outside its platform.

#5157. DOJ Creates 'Super Intelligence' Task Force to Guard Against AI Abuse

DOJ Creates 'Super Intelligence' Task Force to Guard Against AI Abuse

The U.S. Department of Justice is setting up a task force dedicated to policing the misuse of advanced artificial intelligence, as Washington races to build enforcement muscle around a technology it increasingly frames in near-sci-fi terms.

Announced Thursday, the Super Intelligence Task Force, or DOJ-SITF, will be led by Associate Attorney General Stanley Woodward and Associate Deputy Attorney General Stephen Weiss.

Its stated mission is to make sure American leadership in “super intelligence” isn’t compromised by bad actors, whether foreign governments and their proxies, transnational criminal organizations, or companies and individuals using AI to commit fraud or other crimes.

The department invoked President Donald Trump’s framing that “America stands at the forefront of a new technological revolution in intelligence.”

“SI development will continue to be powered by American innovators,” Attorney General Todd Blanche said in a statement, adding that the task force would work alongside SI Force Chair Jay Clayton to keep critical AI technologies from being “manipulated by bad actors at home or abroad.”

Blanche said the DOJ would keep “identifying and prosecuting individuals whose use of SI violates the law, including by maliciously targeting American taxpayers, critical U.S. infrastructure, or other aspects of our national security.”

Notably, the effort isn’t purely about catching criminals. Weiss said the department also has “a responsibility” to “harden regular reporting on SI risk and response, and ensure companies exercise vigorous internal controls so that SI technology behaves as intended,” signaling that the DOJ wants AI developers themselves to tighten safeguards against models going off the rails.

The term “super intelligence” typically refers to a hypothetical AI that surpasses human capability across the board, and the government’s embrace of the phrase marks an escalation in how officials talk about the technology. Former SEC chair Jay Clayton has been tied to a broader Trump administration push around super intelligence, and the SEC recently added “super intelligence” to its 2027 examination priorities.

The task force joins a widening federal scramble to get ahead of AI risk. Trump signed an executive order setting up a fight with states over AI regulation. Earlier this week, the U.S. banking regulator OCC issued its first AI framework as banks battle rogue agents reaching into customer accounts.

The DOJ did not specify a timeline or detail how the task force would coordinate with other agencies, saying only that its work would “promote innovation and prevent the misuse” of the technology.

#5156. XRP’s new euro cash-out route had less than €100 in its direct market

A November 8 snapshot found just 96.80 EURGH in the direct XRP market.

XRP holders seeking euros through GateHub's newly announced EURGH route faced just 96.80 euro tokens in the direct XRP/EURGH order book at a Nov. 8 ledger snapshot.

Wallet platform GateHub announced EURGH with Dinaro, the token's electronic-money issuer, on Nov. 5, saying its euro token on the XRP Ledger and Xahau could be redeemed through SEPA bank payments, subject to legal and verification requirements. For someone starting with XRP, however, obtaining that euro token comes before redeeming it. The measured public market offered little capacity for that first step.

At the validated XRPL ledger that closed Nov. 8 at 13:29:31 UTC, the direct order book returned one offer supplying 96.80 EURGH in exchange for 484 XRP. Its owner's available funds covered the token amount.

That works out to 0.20 EURGH per XRP before additional costs. Even treating each EURGH as one euro, as the issuer's stated redemption terms envisage, the entire displayed token inventory was worth less than €100.

The price was also far from the separate euro market. Kraken's XRP/EUR ticker, captured at 13:27:50 UTC, showed a best bid of €1.04544 per XRP. The observations were 101 seconds apart and covered different venues, before trading, transfer, redemption or banking costs. They were indicative quotes, not a guaranteed cash-out comparison. XRP's broader market was near $1.17 and down 6.70% over seven days in the Nov. 8 afternoon snapshot.

Still, the difference illustrates why a bank connection alone does not establish a competitive exit for XRP. The direct offer asked a seller to accept 0.20 EURGH for each XRP while a separate euro venue displayed a much higher bid. A token's redemption value and the exchange price available to acquire it are separate matters.

The issuer's outstanding XRPL obligations totaled 55,370.62 EURGH at the same ledger. That balance measures issued tokens, not XRP-buying liquidity. Xahau balances are outside this XRPL reading.

There was also no XRP/EURGH automated market maker pool at that ledger. Such a pool would be another place to look for conversion liquidity alongside the direct offers.

A request to convert XRP into 1,000 EURGH returned no route for the tested accounts. The sending account had sufficient XRP, and the receiving account already had room to hold more EURGH on its trust line, the ledger relationship needed to hold that issuer's token.

This was an account-specific routing result, not proof that every possible conversion was unavailable. XRPL's pathfinding documentation cautions that results are not guaranteed to find the best route. The resting offer also describes available terms, rather than a completed trade.

The public XRP-to-EURGH conversion and GateHub's announced EURGH-to-bank service are separate steps.

Bank redemption starts after an XRP holder obtains EURGH. The dated public conversion test found limited inventory and no larger route.

Uses the funded direct offer and account-specific path result at validated ledger 108197128, closed November 8, 2026 at 13:29:31 UTC. The quoted rate is 96.80 EURGH / 484 XRP = 0.20 EURGH per XRP before costs. Connectors show sequence, not scaled amounts.

Dated public liquidity only. Resting offer, not completed trade. No returned path applies to tested accounts. Hosted and negotiated alternatives unmeasured. SEPA service is provider-announced and subject to legal and verification requirements; payouts not independently demonstrated.

Hosted execution needs its own quote. GateHub's Exchange documentation describes market-based rates and immediate execution or cancellation; its Trade guide says Exchange calculates from current market orders. Those interfaces do not, by themselves, demonstrate a separate reserve of EURGH liquidity or a larger executable quote.

GateHub's Xaman on/off-ramp support also describes SEPA and SWIFT connectivity, with a verified GateHub account required. The measured EURGH constraint therefore cannot be extended to all GateHub bank-transfer channels.

GateHub says users can deposit euros by bank transfer and receive EURGH in their selected wallet. Dinaro is the issuer behind that token; GateHub describes its redemption into SEPA payments, subject to legal and verification requirements. Dinaro appears in Banka Slovenije's electronic-money institution register; that institutional entry does not separately identify EURGH.

The announced deposit flow does not itself require a large XRP trading position. XRP remains relevant for account reserves and transaction fees, while traders may choose to hold it as conversion inventory.

XRPL's auto-bridging mechanism can route between two issued tokens through XRP when suitable offers improve the exchange rate. It cannot create the missing inventory, and its existence does not demonstrate lasting demand to hold XRP.

For this euro route to become a more useful exit for XRP holders, the next evidence would be competitive funded quotes, successful larger conversions and attributable production usage. The Nov. 8 public snapshot exposed the conversion hurdle; GateHub's banking announcement addresses the redemption step after it.

#5151. Leios scaling threatens Cardano stability unless fast path passes

Cardano is weighing a faster emergency shutdown for Leios during severe network failures, pending constitutional changes and network tests.

Cardano could give governance a faster way to disable its proposed Leios scaling system during a serious network problem while keeping restarts under normal approval rules.

A Nov. 6 proposal from Kevin Hammond would relax notice and frequency limits for emergency deactivation, without removing the votes or enactment steps needed to change the protocol.

Hammond set out the approach in the Cardano Amendment Proposal 12 (CAP-12) technical discussion, calling it a proposed Technical Steering Committee position ahead of a Nov. 18 review. CAP-12 is a proposed constitutional amendment setting limits for Dijkstra-era protocol parameters.

CAP-12 version 5, which remained open on Nov. 8, prohibits zero-valued Leios parameters, and Hammond’s network-wide shutdown route would use one.

The proposed switch is intended to disable Leios’s additional transaction-processing path while preserving ordinary block production. It would set maxEndorserBlockTxsSize, the limit on transaction payload endorsed by a supplementary block, to zero.

These endorser blocks reference additional transactions, which enter the chain after certification and inclusion of the certificate in a later ranking block.

For a Severity 1 or Severity 2 network issue, Hammond’s fast path would waive the PARAM-04a notice requirement and permit the transition to zero in any epoch.

Switching Leios back on would follow the normal process and remain subject to the network-group frequency limit.

Hammond said ratification and enactment would still take at least one epoch boundary, describing a boundary the action must pass.

Proposed emergency exceptions would ease switch-off; normal parameter-change rules would still apply to restart.

Notice and frequency exceptions would stop at deactivation.

Proposed, not enacted. CAP-12 v5 still forbids zero; behavioral confirmation and switching/conformance tests are prerequisites, with completion unconfirmed in reviewed records.

Qualitative route diagram drawn from CAP-12 and Hammond’s November 6 proposed TSC position. No elapsed-time scale, computed quantities or adopted network behavior is implied.

Proposed, not enacted. CAP-12 v5 still forbids zero; behavioral confirmation and switching/conformance tests are prerequisites, with completion unconfirmed in reviewed records.

CAP-12’s analysis says Leios parameter changes require delegated representatives, the Constitutional Committee and a stake-pool-operator vote. Hammond separately distinguished that governance-controlled protocol switch from an operational switch based on ad-hoc operator participation.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Hammond made the protocol route conditional on written confirmation from the Linear Leios team about what zero does. No endorser blocks should be announced, no votes cast or diffused, and no certificates included in ranking blocks.

Node behavior should be equivalent to Praos, Cardano’s existing block-production system.

He also required switch-off and switch-back-on exercises on the Preview and Preprod test networks, with block production measured against the Praos baseline, plus conformance tests for the zero setting across all node implementations.

Constitutional permission to set a value would still need a matching implementation: clients must interpret zero consistently, and switching back on must work as intended.

Meanwhile, version 5’s MEBTS-09 rule expressly says the parameter must not be zero. Intersect’s Nov. 6 update confirms that the off switch was removed from the constitutional text, with deactivation left to the hard-fork plan and another draft expected.

Adopting Hammond’s route would require reconciling that ban with the proposed exception.

The Nov. 3 working-group minutes say the test network entered the Dijkstra era on Nov. 2 using node 11.2, without full Leios implementation.

Intersect said on Nov. 6 that DijkstraNet supported BLS key registration but not full Linear Leios. Full Leios was available for early integration testing on Musashi, while the combined node 11.3 release candidate was estimated to be at least two to three weeks away.

The Nov. 3 consensus update reports endorser-block forging, certificate checks, and vote relaying. Essential Cardano’s Nov. 6 summary describes those main-branch changes as development work rather than a mainnet release.

The working-group minutes record an aim to submit the constitution and guardrails by Nov. 25.

Hammond also said on Nov. 6 that the Parameter Committee had not yet seen the pending numerical values and would need time to review them.

#5148. OpenAI Promises to Foot AI’s Power Bill After Voter Revolt. Who Enforces It?

OpenAI Promises to Foot AI’s Power Bill After Voter Revolt. Who Enforces It?

OpenAI promised Thursday that neighbors of its future AI campuses won't foot the bill for their electricity needs, two days after voters dealt data center expansion a blow at the polls. It also pledged public energy and water figures and limits on secrecy agreements with officials.

Its National Community Compact covers future campuses (a fancy rebranding for the term AI data centers) OpenAI both owns and operates, according to the company’s announcement. A separate policy blueprint asks legislators and regulators to apply similar requirements across the industry.

“We will pay the full cost of powering our compute campuses and ensure local residents do not foot the bill for our energy needs,” OpenAI said in its “national community compact.”

It also promises financial assurances before costly grid upgrades, so other customers aren't left paying if projects shrink or fail. Each AI data center would get a water plan, annual progress reports and independent noise studies.

The national compact does not describe penalties for noncompliance, a dispute process or independent verification of electricity and water figures. Residents could read OpenAI’s disclosures, but the document does not explain how they could compel corrections or recover losses.

The ownership restriction matters, too. Partners including Oracle build and own much of the infrastructure reserved for OpenAI, Reuters reported. The company has not said its national compact automatically covers those partner-owned facilities.

The proposed blueprint goes further on electricity costs. “Policymakers should direct state public utility commissions to create large-load tariffs that require large electricity consumers to pay the incremental costs they create,” it says.

A large-load tariff is a special electricity rate for major users such as data centers. The blueprint also urges mandatory reporting, narrow limits on officials’ NDAs and provisions letting governments reclaim tax breaks when developers miss agreed commitments.

The announcement follows a bruising election for the industry. Voters backed restrictions on data centers in multiple states, while candidates pushing for tighter oversight won key races. The results basically send a message to OpenAI and the broader AI industry that communities want a bigger say in how these facilities are built and how their operations affect the people living nearby.

OpenAI says every future campus it owns and operates will receive its own local compact and an annual public progress report.

#5146. Amundi puts European stocks on Ethereum, but access terms stay hidden

The share class announced Nov. 5 represents fund units, while Amundi's release leaves investor access, transfer and redemption terms unspecified.

Amundi has issued a tokenized share class of its Amundi Core STOXX Europe 600 equity index fund on public Ethereum on Nov. 5, adding a blockchain route to European stock-market exposure with CACEIS providing the technology and infrastructure for tokenizing the fund units

Amundi said the new class remains integrated into the fund's existing operating model, and investors can also continue accessing the fund through traditional channels.

The token represents a unit in an investment fund, with investors gaining exposure through that fund.

Amundi's Ethereum share class adds a digital route to Core STOXX Europe 600. Traditional channels remain available.

The token represents fund units, not separately issued shares in every underlying company.

The announcement leaves access, transfer and redemption terms unspecified. Connections show fund-unit exposure, not a cash flow or settlement sequence.

The issuance establishes a fund-unit route onto Ethereum, with practical access dependent on the new class's terms.

#5142. Canada opens bank stablecoin access, but strips deposit guarantees

Canada's budget would require banks to issue stablecoins through separate subsidiaries, bringing them under the Stablecoin Act.

Canada's Nov. 5 budget proposes requiring banks and other federally regulated financial institutions that issue stablecoins to do so through separate subsidiaries, bringing those issuers under the Stablecoin Act alongside nonbank issuers.

The proposed subsidiaries would be non-financial institutions regulated under the Act. Budget 2026 also proposes extending the framework to foreign financial institutions that are not prudentially regulated in Canada, with consistent treatment across issuers as its stated goal.

A stablecoin issued by a bank-owned company would still fall within a framework that separates covered tokens from publicly insured deposits.

The legislative annex proposes amending the Bank Act, Trust and Loan Companies Act, and Insurance Companies Act to prohibit federally regulated financial institutions from issuing stablecoins directly.

The existing Stablecoin Act excludes financial-institution issuers, subject to regulations. Routing issuance through a non-financial-institution subsidiary would bring that issuing entity within the stablecoin framework.

The budget would bring bank subsidiaries and certain foreign financial institutions under the Stablecoin Act. These are proposed changes; the operating framework awaits commencement.

Bank ownership would not turn a covered stablecoin into a publicly insured deposit.

Budget intentions require legislation. Justice Canada's consolidation current to October 19 marks operating provisions not in force. Regulatory terms and discretionary exemptions matter; the diagram is not a finding about any named issuer.

Source-backed qualitative relationship diagram. Arrows show proposed issuing routes or scope, not fund transfers or quantitative scale. No forecasts or calculations.

Budget intentions require legislation. Justice Canada's consolidation current to October 19 marks operating provisions not in force. Regulatory terms and discretionary exemptions matter; the diagram is not a finding about any named issuer.

The Bank of Canada says its role under the Act will include registering non-financial institution issuers, supervising compliance, monitoring issuance and redemption risks, and taking enforcement action.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The planned foreign-institution extension targets institutions outside Canadian prudential supervision. The annex also proposes clarifying that stablecoin issuance is not deposit-taking for foreign banks and their affiliates covered by Part XII of the Bank Act.

The existing Act separately excludes central banks, and also lets the Bank of Canada Governor grant conditional exemptions where provincial or foreign rules are substantially similar.

Once applicable, the Act's safeguards would require redemption at face value in the currency a stablecoin tracks.

It would also require reserves worth at least the outstanding face value, and qualified custody that segregates reserve assets and protects them from issuer and custodian creditor claims other than holders' redemption claims. Those duties remain subject to the Act's statutory and regulatory terms.

Sections 33 and 34 would prohibit covered stablecoins from being deposits, publicly deposit-insured instruments or government-guaranteed or backstopped instruments, and prohibit representations suggesting those features.

A bank parent's involvement would not convert the token into an insured savings balance.

The budget also promises continued engagement on stronger consumer protections if an issuer or custodian becomes insolvent.

The Act received royal assent on March 26, but its operating provisions take effect on dates set by order of the Governor in Council.

The Oct. 24 proposed-regulations impact statement identifies fall 2027 as the intended commencement window for the wider framework.

The budget's new development concerns who would issue within the framework, while implementation still depends on legislation and commencement.

#5139. Bitcoin Fixes This? Paraguay President Resigns After Bank Lockout Hits 60% of Country

Bitcoin Fixes This? Paraguay President Resigns After Bank Lockout Hits 60% of Country

Paraguayan President Santiago Peña announced his resignation Thursday after the collapse of digital lender Ueno Bank left roughly three million customers unable to access their accounts, turning a banking failure into a political emergency.

The announcement did not end the confrontation. Paraguay’s lower house voted 78-0 to impeach Peña minutes later, sending the charges to the Senate as Vice President Pedro Alliana prepared to take over, per AP.

The bank’s shutdown has also revived an argument familiar to Bitcoin holders: Money held by a financial intermediary is only as accessible as that institution allows. Self-custodied Bitcoin works differently, although it comes with risks of its own.

As the Bitcoin OGs would say, “Bitcoin fixes this.”

Paraguay’s central bank placed Ueno into resolution after regulators found its solvency ratio had fallen below half the legal minimum. The process, announced on November 3, followed accounting adjustments to the bank’s financial statements and left its app, cards, and transfers out of service.

Ueno claimed around 2.9 million customers, more than 60% of Paraguay’s adults. It also held roughly $800 million in public-sector deposits, including pension and health funds, according to AP, complicating an already painful freeze on ordinary savings and wages.

Peña previously held a stake in a company linked to Ueno’s owners, a relationship that fueled allegations of conflicts of interest. He has denied wrongdoing and said he sold his interest in March 2025, according to Reuters.

“My greatest wish is that the return of those funds be guaranteed, that the Deposit Guarantee Fund fulfill its purpose,” Peña said in his resignation address. The Deposit Guarantee Fund would be the equivalent of the FDIC in the United States.

Mensaje del Presidente de la República, Santiago Peña, al pueblo paraguayo. pic.twitter.com/RBz6IfFV9L

— Santiago Peña (@SantiPenap) November 5, 2026

Outside Congress, the anger was less diplomatic. “Santiago Peña and the Colorado Party have stolen all our savings,” protester Luis Pineda told AP. “We are fed up with 70 years of plunder.”

The central bank says Ueno’s failure is an isolated case, not evidence of a broader banking collapse. It has announced a deposit guarantee of up to 228.3 million guaraníes per depositor, roughly $36,000, with initial payments targeting eligible accounts holding no more than 10 million guaraníes.

Bitcoin was born during another banking crisis. Its first block, mined in January 2009, included a newspaper headline about a second bailout for British banks, a historical detail long cited by supporters who distrust reliance on financial intermediaries.

A Bitcoin holder who controls a wallet’s private keys can authorize transfers from the wallet without asking a third party, like a bank, to unlock an account. That is the principle behind the crypto saying “not your keys, not your coins,” and the distinction from leaving Bitcoin on an exchange that can itself freeze withdrawals.

But Bitcoin is not perfect. Self-custody has its own risks: Forget your keys or lose them and there is no way you can recover your funds, there is nobody who can assist you, and no law enforcement agency who can do anything for you.

Paraguay’s central bank said Thursday its first guarantee payments would go to depositors with up to 10 million guaraníes, no Ueno debts and an account at another financial institution.

#5133. US Treasury cuts off MEXC over alleged $16B in illicit transactions

The US Treasury bureau FinCEN on Friday ordered US financial institutions to stop processing transfers involving MEXC Global.

The US Department of Treasury on Friday issued an order to cut cryptocurrency exchange MEXC Global off from the US’ financial system after accusing it of facilitating at least $16 billion in transactions linked to sanctions evasion, cybercrime and other illicit activity.

The Treasury’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) asserted that MEXC handled transactions involving sanctioned groups in Iran, Russia and North Korea between May 2021 and May 2026. This included around $1.5 billion in transactions linked to Russian sanctions evasion, according to the official press release.

The order effectively bans US financial institutions from processing money transfers involving MEXC and will take effect 30 days after it is published in the Federal Register.

“Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system,” said Secretary of the Treasury Scott Bessent. “Treasury will continue to sever the Iranian regime’s economic lifelines and thwart its desperate attempts to circumvent U.S. sanctions using digital assets.”

The action is part of Operation Economic Outcast, a US Treasury campaign launched in August with aims to cut off financial entities that help Iran evade sanctions.

According to DefiLlama, the exchange held about $4.48 billion in total assets as of Friday. Cointelegraph contacted MEXC for comment but had not received a response by publication.

#5131. Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Anthropic launched a personal finance feature for Claude on Thursday, letting users connect bank accounts and ask the chatbot what they have spent, what bills are due, and how much money they have left.

The announcement came with an accusation from crypto investor Anthony Pompliano, who says Anthropic tried to get inside information from his competing startup, Silvia, months before the rollout.

Claude can then summarize spending, spot duplicate charges or unexpected fees, explain interest payments, and build charts or bill calendars. Anthropic's example shows a user asking where their money went this month, then following up to find out what remains before payday.

"Access is read-only, and Claude can't move, manage, or invest your money," Anthropic wrote on X. "Like the rest of Claude, Personal Finance has no ads or sponsored recommendations," the company added.

The assistant can see balances, credit limits, account names and types, the last four digits of account numbers, and up to 24 months of transactions from accounts users approve. According to Anthropic's documentation, conversations inside the Personal Finance hub are not used to train its models, but account balances may lag and AI-generated figures should be checked against bank records.

Also, how Anthropic tracks its users’ financial data and uses it beyond model training is not completely clear—and this is extremely valuable information for big data companies.

Pompliano, whose Silvia product bills itself as a personal AI chief financial officer, posted a lengthy complaint on X shortly after Claude's announcement. He alleged that Anthropic personnel began approaching Silvia engineers in April, supposedly because they were interested in the startup's growth and computing needs.

"Anthropic was fishing for details on how we built our product and why it was so popular," he wrote. Pompliano also described an alleged steak dinner involving Anthropic representatives, claiming someone told the Silvia team that the meal was on CEO Dario Amodei's tab.

His engineers declined to divulge sensitive information, he claimed, adding that someone at Anthropic warned his team about the upcoming launch days earlier.

"Today, Anthropic launched a Claude wrapper that is masquerading as a personal finance product," Pompliano wrote. He also claimed Silvia's computing costs are roughly 97% lower when using its own models and hardware instead of frontier AI models, which most likely mean they are using a local model, likely finetuned for the startup’s specific purpose.

That said, Anthropic was not the first general-purpose chatbot maker to move into personal finance, either. OpenAI launched similar bank connections for ChatGPT in May, also using Plaid for read-only access to financial information, and Agents like Hermes, Open Claw, or Grok Bot can also do pretty similar tasks given the proper skills, permissions, and instructions.

Anthropic says Personal Finance is a first step and is exploring partnerships with licensed professionals and regulated financial firms. For now, the beta is restricted to U.S. customers on three paid Claude plans.

#5129. Coinbase Wins Full Federal Trust Charter From OCC

Coinbase has won a full national trust bank charter from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency, upgrading the conditional approval it received in April and putting its institutional custody business under direct federal supervision, the exchange said Monday.

The OCC approved the application for Coinbase National Trust Company last Friday, the company said in a blog post. OCC licensing records, as reported, show the chartering process was completed Nov. 6.

Coinbase applied in October 2025 and received preliminary approval on April 2. In the intervening months it had to finish its organizational and operational preparations before the OCC would let the trust bank open.

“This charter allows us to bring clear and consistent federal uniformity to our industry-leading custody capabilities,” Coinbase wrote, adding that it strengthens the regulatory framework under which it serves customers. The company said it has served as a custodian for more than 14 years.

A national trust bank can hold assets for clients as a fiduciary, and Coinbase’s focus is custody for institutional customers. It is not a commercial bank. It cannot take ordinary retail deposits or make loans, and the charter does not authorize it to issue stablecoins.

Staking, prime trading and financing will keep running through Coinbase affiliates, not the trust itself. Under the OCC’s April decision, the trust bank acts as an intermediary for those services and does not perform them directly.

The decision also sets conditions. The trust bank must hold at least $60 million in Tier 1 capital and enough liquid assets to cover 180 days of operating expenses during its first three years.

The license does not automatically connect Coinbase to Federal Reserve payment systems either. That requires a separate application under the Fed’s account access guidelines.

Coinbase already runs custody through Coinbase Custody Trust Company, a limited-purpose trust regulated by the New York State Department of Financial Services. The company plans to move that business to the national entity over three years.

Coinbase is the latest crypto firm to complete a federal trust charter. Ripple received final approval on Oct. 26, and Circle won final OCC approval in July, a decision that put USDC reserves on a path toward federal supervision.

The OCC has been courting the sector. In August, Comptroller Jonathan V. Gould said “America and the OCC are once again open for business,” as the regulator touted its charter push. Ripple, Circle, Crypto.com and Paxos had all received conditional approval by then. Block applied for its own banking charter in September.

The approval did not come without resistance. The Independent Community Bankers of America urged the OCC in December to reject Coinbase’s application, arguing the exchange has “demonstrably flawed risk and control functions.” The American Bankers Association asked the regulator in February to slow its review of crypto charter requests.

Underneath those objections is a turf war over stablecoins. Coinbase wants to pay customers rewards for holding the tokens, and banks say that could erode their deposit base.

Coinbase (COIN) did not disclose any financial impact from the charter in its announcement, which framed the approval as a regulatory milestone.

The timing is notable. The approval arrives days after Democrats won both chambers of Congress, and a charter issued by an executive-branch regulator sits outside any legislative fight, at least for now. Vice Chairman Ryan VanGrack said the company has spent years “asking for clear rules and rigorous oversight.” The company also said the approval is “another major step” in integrating digital assets into the American financial system.

The next test is whether Coinbase moves its existing custody clients over on schedule, and whether the community bankers who opposed the charter take their objections to court or to Congress.

#5124. Strive (ASST) Adds 29 Bitcoin for $2.2 Million as Weekly Buys Slow After Crossing 43,000

Strive bought another 29 bitcoin last week for about $2.2 million at an average of $75,721 per coin between Oct. 31 and Nov. 6, a Monday 8-K filing shows, lifting total holdings to 43,153 BTC from 43,124 as of Oct. 30 and capping a month that had already pushed the Dallas treasury past 40,000 coins.

Chairman and CEO Matthew Cole posted the same figures on X: “Strive acquired 29 $BTC for $2.2M at an average cost of $75,721 per bitcoin, bringing total holdings to ₿43,153.” Strive’s corporate account republished the note minutes later. The average price is inclusive of fees and expenses, the filing says.

The buy is a sharp downshift from the pace Strive set through October. The prior week alone, from Oct. 24 through Oct. 30, Strive added 124 bitcoin at about $80,526 a coin. Between Oct. 1 and Oct. 30 the company acquired 15,124 bitcoin in total, and Friday’s third-quarter results put year-to-date bitcoin yield — Strive’s measure of the percentage change in bitcoin per share — at 63.2% through Sept. 30, with an 18.5% print for the third quarter alone.

Holdings stood at 28,000 bitcoin on Sept. 30 before the October sprint. That climb followed multi-thousand-coin buys earlier this fall, including a 6,182-coin purchase funded with preferred proceeds, and kept Strive among the largest public corporate stacks after Strategy.

Cash and cash equivalents fell to $487.6 million as of Nov. 6 from $490.4 million a week earlier, a $2.8 million drop that roughly covers the roughly $2.2 million bitcoin spend plus fees. Strive did not break out a separate funding line for this week’s coins in the 8-K.

The preferred stack kept moving. Shares of Strive’s 15% Series A perpetual preferred (SATS) rose by 33,141 to 7,036,559, a tally that includes shares sold through 4:00 p.m. Eastern that settle the next business day. SATA preferred stock was unchanged at 14,030,176. Effective common shares outstanding held flat at 127,331,109 after a Class A increase of 15,382 was offset by a matching Class B decline.

Strive still holds 505,000 shares of Strategy’s variable-rate STRC preferred, marked at a fair value of $50.4 million as of Nov. 6, up slightly from $50.1 million a week earlier. The company closed a registered offering of 7 million SATS shares on Oct. 20 for about $609 million in net proceeds and later lined up a $1 billion SATS at-the-market program, tools that have funded the larger buys that built the stack and that still leave room to keep adding if the ATM finds buyers.

At bitcoin near $74,650, the 43,153-coin treasury is worth roughly $3.22 billion on paper. That marks the stack about 1.4% under this week’s $75,721 average purchase price, though Strive has not disclosed a companywide cost basis in Monday’s filing the way Strategy does each week.

The Strive update lands on the same Monday that Strategy disclosed its own weekly purchase. Strategy added 1,036 bitcoin for $81.9 million at an average of $79,012, lifting its holdings to 850,364 BTC bought for $64.16 billion at an average cost of $75,456. The two filings keep the familiar pairing in view: Strategy still dwarfs every other public corporate holder, while Strive has used preferred issuance to climb the treasury rankings with a faster bitcoin-per-share growth claim.

ASST closed Friday at $21.45, up from Thursday’s $20.59 close and still well below its Oct. 21 local high near $30.75. The 52-week range runs from $7.02 to $34.40. At the time of writing, bitcoin is near $74,650, Bitcoin Magazine Pro data shows. The test for Strive from here is whether another week of single-digit coin adds is a pause after October’s sprint or the new run rate once the preferred ATM is the main fuel.

#5123. Zcash's NU7 Upgrade Goes Live, Recycling 60% of Fees to Fund Future Block Rewards

Zcash's NU7 Upgrade Goes Live, Recycling 60% of Fees to Fund Future Block Rewards

"NU7 has successfully activated on mainnet. A new era of Zcash is here," the project's official account tweeted.

NU7 has successfully activated on mainnet. A new era of Zcash is here. $ZEC pic.twitter.com/rPaSFe9h7j

"This is a huge milestone for Shielded Labs," Jason McGee of Shielded Labs, the Zcash development organization behind the mechanism, tweeted ahead of the activation.

The NSM tackles a problem Zcash inherited from Bitcoin: whether, as block subsidies decline with each halving, transaction fees alone will be enough to pay miners to secure the network. Under the mechanism, 60% of transaction fees will be removed from circulation and gradually reissued through future block rewards, while preserving the 21 million ZEC supply cap. Reissuance will begin in 2031, with the recycled funds spread along a smooth curve on top of the existing halving schedule.

The NSM was Shielded Labs' first project and builds on an idea that developer Nate Wilcox proposed years ago, originally called the Zcash Posterity Fund, McGee said. A separate proposal under consideration would require Zcash Community Grants to contribute 50,000 ZEC from its treasury to the NSM.

— Jason McGee 🛡 (@aquietinvestor) November 5, 2026

NU7 also limits how much private transaction activity can fit into each block, reducing the data wallets must scan to find their payments, according to the upgrade specification.

Shielded Labs' original design would have replaced Zcash's halvings with a smooth issuance curve. Community panels supported that change, but coinholders did not, so an alternative design that kept the halvings was developed instead.

"We still think the original design was simpler and better, but we respect the process that led us here," McGee wrote. "It should be difficult to change the protocol, and the process should resist capture by any single organization, group, or influential individual."

The upgrade arrives as Zcash's community debates the future of Zcash Community Grants (ZCG) and its share of the Dev Fund, the slice of block rewards that pays for development.

The five organizations that coordinate Zcash's coinholder voting, Project Tachyon, Shielded Labs, Valar Group, the Zcash Foundation and ZODL, have set out an expedited timeline for proposals and polling, saying "tensions were high" and that a drawn-out debate risked "deepening divisions within the Zcash community."

Proposals are due by November 8, with polls for coinholders and community panels running from November 16 to November 30. If rough consensus emerges, the groups will support a further network upgrade, NU 7.1, to implement it.

#5116. Web3Auth says it frontran hackers by moving $5.6 million out of exposed Keplr wallets

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Consensys' Web3Auth team said it used a flaw that exposed some Keplr Wallet users' private keys to move their funds to safe addresses. Its 12 recovery wallets currently hold about $5.6 million.

Web3Auth, the Consensys-owned service that runs Google and Apple sign-in for crypto wallet Keplr, said it moved funds out of vulnerable Keplr wallets to keep them away from an attacker. The 12 addresses it says hold the recovered funds had about $5.6 million in crypto as of publicatino time on Sunday, according to onchain data reviewed by The Block.

A flaw in Web3Auth's systems exposed the private keys of some Keplr Wallet users who signed up with a Google or Apple account, the firms said. Though it was initially designed for the Cosmos ecosystem, Keplr now supports now supports many non-Cosmos chains, including EVM networks, and said in 2025 that it had more than 2 million users.

"This was not a hack of Keplr's code. The incident originated at Web3Auth, a provider Keplr uses," Keplr co-founder Josh Lee wrote on X, estimating that about 20% of Keplr's Google and Apple login accounts were affected. Users who set up Keplr with a recovery phrase or a hardware wallet are not affected, Keplr said.

Web3Auth said on Saturday it had started emptying vulnerable wallets the attacker hadn't reached yet, using "the same path of the original attacker." "Funds recovered through this process are safe, and we are working with Keplr on a path to recovery for users," Web3Auth said.

Web3Auth's reference to an "original attacker" suggests some wallets were drained before the recovery began. Web3Auth has not said how many wallets the attacker hit or how much was taken, and did not immediately respond to a request for further information from The Block.

"We are aware that some of these transactions have been mistaken for attacker activity," Web3Auth said early Sunday, when it published its recovery addresses. Blockchain analytics firm Bitquery had reported on Saturday that a thief collected about $6 million from more than 44,000 Keplr wallets. All eight addresses Bitquery tied to the thief are on Web3Auth's recovery list.

Most of the recovered funds are on Cosmos Hub, where Web3Auth's recovery address holds about 2.49 million ATOM Cosmos Hub (ATOM), worth roughly $3.3 million. Much of that ATOM had been staked, and the sweeps converted it into tokens that could be moved right away instead of waiting out the network's 21-day withdrawal period.

Lee said on Saturday that many vulnerable accounts may have dodged the attack. "It appears only a fraction of that 20% has actually been exploited, and the remaining accounts may be recoverable," he wrote. Keplr warned that some accounts "may already show a reduced or empty balance as a result of these mitigation efforts."

Keplr told Google and Apple login users not to send any funds to their accounts, advising them to move any remaining funds to a wallet secured by a recovery phrase or a hardware device. Keplr said it is working with Web3Auth on a way for users to check whether their accounts were affected.

Web3Auth first disclosed the issue on Friday afternoon and paused the service that lets apps create wallets through social logins. It said it had found no evidence that users of MetaMask, Consensys' flagship wallet, were affected. Consensys acquired Web3Auth in June 2025 and now markets the service as MetaMask Embedded Wallets, according to its developer documentation.

The Block has reached out to Web3Auth, Consensys and Keplr for comment.

#5114. Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

A former FBI agent has pleaded guilty to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency using wallet credentials the bureau had collected during its own investigations, the Justice Department said on Thursday.

Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, pleaded guilty to wire fraud and money laundering in federal court in Alexandria. He faces up to 20 years in prison and is scheduled to be sentenced on March 4, 2027.

Former FBI agent Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, pled guilty today to wire fraud and money laundering related to using his access to steal cryptocurrency.@FBIWFO https://t.co/jDVtSiAM3H

— U.S. Attorney EDVA (@EDVAnews) November 5, 2026

Yaroch worked at the FBI's Boston Division from 2017 to early 2025 on a squad investigating an unnamed "adversarial nation state," before moving to the bureau's Counterintelligence and Espionage Division, where he had access to FBI systems holding crypto credentials gathered in investigations, according to prosecutors.

Beginning no later than December 2024, he used that access to steal crypto from wallets controlled by subjects of other investigations, by nation-state actors, and from at least one wallet the FBI had created for an operation. He made about 100 fraudulent transfers using credentials from 35 separate FBI investigations, then swapped the stolen funds into other cryptocurrencies and moved them through further transactions to hide their origin. He used some of the money to pay off credit cards, car payments and other loans.

Yaroch also planned a new life abroad. He asked ChatGPT how to maximize profits from investments and where to live in the European Union, and after the chatbot suggested buying a vineyard in Portugal, he gave Portuguese lawyers power of attorney to obtain a tax identification number for him, prosecutors said. He arranged to travel to Portugal in September.

His actions compromised multiple FBI investigations into criminal groups and nation-state adversaries, as well as sources and methods used in foreign intelligence collection, according to the plea agreement's statement of facts.

Yaroch turned himself in. On July 29, he broke down while telling a former colleague at the Justice Department's National Security Division that he had made "very poor decisions" involving crypto wallets, according to an FBI affidavit filed in the case. He then self-reported to FBI headquarters, saying he had become frustrated that the bureau "could not or would not act" against an adversary's crypto accounts and had decided to "take matters into his own hands."

At the time, Yaroch said he had made about 10 to 12 transfers worth under $1 million, far less than the figures in his plea. Agents searching his home two days later seized a Trezor hardware wallet, three passports and a slip of paper bearing seed phrases, according to the affidavit. His Kraken account held about $188,600, mostly in U.S. dollars and the USDC stablecoin, with smaller amounts in Squid (QUID) and Bitcoin. A further $933,800 sat in Suilend, a lending protocol on the Sui blockchain, which he had accessed through the Slush wallet app. Yaroch told agents he chose Suilend "simply because he liked that the logo was a water droplet."

Agents transferred about $925,000 from those accounts to government-controlled wallets, the affidavit said. The roughly $165,600 held as U.S. dollars on Kraken could not be moved the same way.

Decrypt reported on the charges against Yaroch in August.

#5113. U.S. Regulators Drop $250 Travel Rule Plan, Crypto Language Too

FinCEN and the Federal Reserve have formally withdrawn a 2020 proposal that would have cut the Bank Secrecy Act threshold for cross-border funds transfers from $3,000 to $250 and spelled out that the rules cover crypto, leaving the existing $3,000 line in place.

The notice, published Monday in the Federal Register, says the Financial Crimes Enforcement Network and the Fed’s Board of Governors “will not take further action” on a proposal they first issued on Oct. 27, 2020.

That plan would have lowered the recordkeeping and Travel Rule thresholds to $250 for transfers that begin or end outside the United States. Those rules require banks and nonbank financial institutions to collect, retain and pass along customer information on transfers of $3,000 or more.

It also would have clarified the meaning of “money” in both rules so they apply to transactions involving convertible virtual currency, along with digital assets that have legal tender status to a recipient.

Per the notcie, FinCEN and the Fed received nearly 3,000 comments on the proposal, most of them substantially similar form letters. Commenters generally opposed the lower threshold, arguing that it would burden smaller institutions and could push consumers sending remittances toward less regulated channels.

Many also noted that potentially illicit transfers below $3,000 were already being flagged to FinCEN through suspicious activity reports. Several said the threshold should rise instead, citing inflation since 1995.

Sentiment on the crypto language was mixed. Companies that provide crypto services and their trade groups opposed it, citing technical limits and the lack of an international messaging standard for crypto transfers, while some banks and money services businesses supported it.

The agencies gave two reasons for pulling the proposal. More than five years have passed, and they said more public feedback would help before adopting anything similar. The withdrawal, they wrote, gives the public “certainty” that no final rule will be issued without another chance to comment.

The second reason is the crypto industry itself. The notice says crypto markets “have continued to mature” and new legal frameworks are emerging, citing the GENIUS Act stablecoin law enacted on July 18, 2025. Any future change tied to crypto, the agencies said, should be considered in light of those developments so it is “fit for purpose.”

The notice does not repeal or amend any rule. The $3,000 threshold that has applied for decades stays where it is, and so does the existing Travel Rule.

What disappears is the specific path to $250 and the proposed wording that would have written crypto into the definition of money. The agencies did not say whether they plan a new proposal or offer a timeline.

It does not signal a retreat from enforcement. On Friday, FinCEN cut off the MEXC exchange from the U.S. financial system over alleged sanctions evasion, using a different legal tool aimed at one named platform instead of a rule covering every institution.

Stablecoins are following their own track. Under the GENIUS Act, the Fed in June proposed customer identification requirements for stablecoin issuers, and FinCEN and the Treasury’s sanctions office proposed anti-money-laundering programs for the same firms in April.

For bitcoin users, the practical effect is that nothing changes: the $3,000 trigger stays, and the crypto-specific wording that industry groups opposed is gone for now.

Whether FinCEN and the Fed return with a new threshold, a crypto definition or neither will not be clear until one is published, and the agencies have committed to taking comments first.

#5111. Suspicious prediction market activity seen before election poll release: NYT

Bets were placed shortly before two recent New York Times/Siena political polls were released to the public, according to a report.

Suspicious prediction market bets were placed shortly before two recent New York Times/Siena political polls were released to the public, according to a report.

Vanderbilt University researchers said a Polymarket user bet almost $200,000 on Oct. 31 that Nebraska Senator Pete Ricketts would be re-elected. An hour later, a Times/Siena poll was released showing Ricketts in the lead.

A Friday New York Times report said while it was not clear if the user had access to inside information, they won more than $77,000.

The user also placed similar large bets on other markets, including Kalshi, the report said.

“There’s a series of circumstances here that it’s hard to write it off as a random occurrence,” said Brett Goldstein of the Vanderbilt Institute of National Security. It “requires further investigation.”

Polymarket said it had spotted the trades and was investigating. Kalshi declined comment.

Cointelegraph reported in April that a US Army soldier had been charged after making more than $400,000 by betting on a prediction market allegedly using nonpublic information tied to a US operation involving then Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro.

In a statement, the Department of Justice alleged that Master Sergeant Gannon Ken Van Dyke was involved in the planning and execution of Operation Absolute Resolve, which led to the capture of Maduro at a residence in Caracas, Venezuela, in January.

Van Dyke allegedly used this information to buy “yes” shares in related Polymarket contracts, including “Maduro out by January 31” and “Trump invokes War Powers against Venezuela by January 31,” according to the DOJ.

#5110. Onchain data casts doubt on insider-trading theory in NYT’s ‘suspicious’ election betting story

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

A New York Times story raised questions about a Polymarket trader's winning Senate bets, but The Block's analysis reveals a broader trading history that suggests other explanations for the wagers.

The New York Times wrote Friday that Vanderbilt University researchers had flagged large, profitable bets on Polymarket and Kalshi that were placed before certain polls on the Nebraska and Michigan Senate races were released. The polls favored the candidates the trader had backed, raising questions about whether the trader had advance access to the results.

However, The Block's own analysis of onchain records and trading data found that the trader has a long history of both large, risky trades and complex DeFi yield-farming that was unreported by the Times. Matching onchain deposits to Kalshi trades also suggests that the trader may have placed two large bets 10 minutes after one poll in question was published.

The trader held on to the bets all the way to Election Day rather than taking profits from short-term moves in the markets in the wake of the polls, and placed a bet on the race in Michigan while the Times/Siena polling was still underway, facts that the Times acknowledged in its report.

Those facts complicate the theory that the trader's profits resulted from advance access to unpublished Times/Siena polls. Two experts who reviewed The Block's findings said that while foul play can't be definitively ruled out, other explanations, like the trader being a crypto whale with a proclivity for large directional bets, fit the evidence better.

The Times story acknowledged it was unclear whether the trader had inside information. The researchers offered other possible explanations, and Polymarket told the paper it was conducting its own investigation.

Rajiv Sethi, an economics professor at Barnard College who reviewed The Block's findings, said the activity looked more like bets on the eventual election outcomes than an attempt to profit from the publication of the Times/Siena polls.

"I think these were just directional bets. Maybe they were based on non-public information such as privately commissioned surveys, but I don't think the Times/Siena polls were the information source," Sethi said.

The Vanderbilt researchers identified a Polymarket user who bet $191,277 that Sen. Pete Ricketts, a Nebraska Republican, would win re-election. Most of that money was wagered in the early hours of Oct. 31, roughly an hour before a Times/Siena poll published at 5:05 a.m. ET showed Ricketts ahead by six points. The user ultimately made more than $77,000 on the race, according to the Times.

The same user bet $622,608 on a Democratic victory in Michigan's Senate race. At around 7:53 p.m. ET on Nov. 1, the Times published polls of Michigan and six other states showing Michigan Democrat Abdul El-Sayed ahead by 11 points. The user made more than $180,000 on that race when El-Sayed won the seat.

The Block's analysis of Polymarket's public records matched those bets to an account named dubdubdub2. On Oct. 31, it bought $349,135 of "Yes" shares on a Democratic win in Michigan at 3:27 a.m. and $273,473 of "No" shares on a Republican win at 5:20 a.m., which are essentially bets in the same direction. Together, the two bets match the Michigan total reported by the Times.

The bets were unusually large for those markets. The account's Michigan purchases were the largest daily total by one account in any of the 16 Senate races The Block analyzed back to mid-September, and were nearly three times the next largest.

"There's a series of circumstances here that it's hard to write it off as a random occurrence," Brett Goldstein, who co-leads the prediction markets research team at Vanderbilt's Institute of National Security, told the Times. He said the activity "requires further investigation."

But while the Polymarket trades stood out on the platform, The Block's review of the trader's onchain history suggests the size of the bets was not unusual for the trader, and the specific details of some trades cut against the theory that they were exploiting advance knowledge of the polls.

Michigan also wasn't the race the Nov. 1 polls moved most. That release covered seven states, and the biggest market reaction came in Kansas, where Polymarket's odds of a Democratic win jumped from about 52.5% to 60.5% within half an hour. In Michigan, where Democrats were already the strongest favorites at about 74.5%, the odds rose to about 79.5% over two hours.

The trader did not bet on Kansas. Its market was much smaller, however, with about $1.9 million traded on a Democratic win through the election, compared with about $7.9 million in Michigan.

The researchers told the Times that the trader sent large sums to Kalshi and that large bets on similar markets followed, but the report didn't identify those bets.

The Block traced funds from wallets linked to the Polymarket account to a crypto payment provider used by Kalshi, then compared those deposits with Kalshi's public trade records. The records suggest the trader was betting on the same races on both platforms in the same directions, including possible bets placed after the Nebraska poll became public.

The account's Senate bets were funded by a separate wallet that also traded on Hyperliquid. The Block linked that wallet to the one controlling dubdubdub2 through a history of transfers between connected addresses. Its five transfers to dubdubdub2's Polymarket deposit address on Oct. 30 and 31, totaling $850,000, also match the account's deposit records to the dollar.

At 5:29 p.m. ET on Oct. 30, the betting wallet withdrew its remaining $2.2 million from Hyperliquid, where it had been trading over the previous day. About a minute later, it began sending funds to a deposit address that forwards money to a wallet labeled by blockchain analytics firm Arkham and Arbiscan as belonging to Zero Hash.

Kalshi lists Zero Hash among its crypto deposit providers. The betting wallet sent about $1.39 million to the deposit address through Oct. 31. The Block found no earlier transfers to Zero Hash from the trader's known wallets.

Kalshi's data does not link specific trades to specific accounts. However, since the transfers were consistent with Kalshi deposits and six-figure bets were rare in Kalshi's Nebraska and Michigan Senate markets, The Block was able to match certain deposits to trades by timing and size.

Several of the trader's Zero Hash deposits were followed within roughly a minute by large purchases in Kalshi's Nebraska Senate markets. On Oct. 30, a $175,000 deposit at 5:30:08 p.m. was followed 31 seconds later by about $159,000 in purchases backing Ricketts at 66 cents. A further $157,000 deposit at 5:31:58 p.m. was followed at 5:33:29 p.m. by about $169,000 in bets against Osborn at 67 cents. Both positions backed the same election outcome.

At about 5:15 a.m. on Oct. 31, 10 minutes after the Times/Siena poll was published, Kalshi recorded about $153,000 in purchases backing Ricketts to win and independent Dan Osborn to lose. The two groups of trades came eight seconds apart at prices around 75 cents, echoing the paired purchases seen earlier.

These trades are harder to attribute to the trader, however. They came about 50 minutes after a $250,000 deposit, while several earlier purchases followed deposits within roughly a minute. The deposit could also have funded bets in other markets.

If the trader did make those bets, continuing to buy in after the poll was published would suggest it was betting on the election outcome generally rather than trying to profit from the poll's short-term effect on the market, though late buys do not completely rule out the trader having advance knowledge of the poll.

Onchain transfers suggest the trader may have made more from its Kalshi activity than the roughly $258,000 its Polymarket Senate positions earned. On Nov. 4 and 5, its wallet received about $1.98 million from Zero Hash-labeled wallets, roughly $590,000 more than it had sent to Zero Hash in the first place, presumably as Kalshi deposits.

The transfers alone don't show exactly how much the trader made on Kalshi. The difference between the funds deposited and the funds received could reflect winning bets in other markets, trades that are missing from Kalshi's public records or money the trader originally deposited via a bank account.

The Times reported that the user had traded in just three Polymarket markets before October, but its story did not detail what those trades were. The Block's review found those positions were also substantial, and that holding through settlement was a recurring strategy for the trader.

The trader's dubdubdub2 account bet about $1.05 million on Zohran Mamdani winning the 2025 New York City mayoral election, about $102,000 against Andrew Cuomo in the same race and about $131,000 against the San Francisco 49ers winning the 2026 Super Bowl. It held all three positions until the markets resolved. The Block did not identify any bets that were placed shortly before mayoral polls were published.

Vincent Grégoire, a finance professor at HEC Montréal whose research includes insider trading, described the trader's history as "consistent with a wealthy individual (or small trading firm) who likes to take large bets on various platforms."

While the Times story notes that the trades were made early in the morning U.S. time, The Block's review of roughly two years of activity beginning in October 2024 found that 3 a.m. ET was the busiest hour for wallets linked to the trader. The analysis excluded Oct. 29 through Nov. 5, 2026, so the Senate bets themselves did not influence the result. It covered activity on Ethereum, Arbitrum, Base, Polygon, Hyperliquid and Polymarket, plus Solana bridge transfers.

The trader also has extensive experience within decentralized finance outside prediction markets, per The Block's analysis. The linked wallets have repeatedly farmed lending and yield protocols such as Aave and Pendle, adding up to tens of millions of dollars in total transfer volume. They have withdrawn more than $13 million in stablecoins from Kraken since October 2025. A linked Solana wallet also traded memecoins heavily in early 2025, including the official TRUMP token on its launch weekend.

The trader has also seen its share of trades gone wrong. Immediately before the election bets, the betting wallet deposited about $2.99 million in USDC on Hyperliquid and lost about $783,000 over 26 hours, including fees and funding costs, according to Hypurrscan's records for the account. The losses included about $217,000 on HYPE spot trading, $234,000 on a leveraged HYPE long and $311,000 on leveraged Intel perpetual futures positions opened after Intel reported third-quarter results.

The wallet behind dubdubdub2 also bought 60.43 million WLFI tokens for about $906,500 on the first day of the October 2024 public token sale by World Liberty Financial, the crypto venture owned by President Donald Trump's family, a purchase the Times also noted. The purchase made the wallet the largest buyer in the sale that month.

The Times said the purchase accounted for nearly one-third of all tokens sold that October. Onchain records reviewed by The Block instead put its share at about 6% of the roughly 970 million WLFI sold by the project that month, or about 9% of first-day sales. The Block has asked the Times about the discrepancy.

Grégoire said in an interview with The Block that large election bets can reflect a trader's forecasting models, private polls or other sources of information.

"To me, the simplest explanation is that there's no foul play," he said. "It's just someone that has other sources or other models."

Grégoire questioned why a polling employee would place a bet worth hundreds of thousands of dollars ahead of publication and then leave the position open rather than taking the immediate profit. He also noted that some of Polymarket's biggest historical winners made their money through a small number of large presidential election bets.

Sethi also said the use of Kalshi, which verifies traders' identities, suggests "they were not too concerned with concealing their identity."

Though The Block's analysis casts doubt on the idea that the trader had advance access to the Times/Siena polls, the reasons for the trader's bets cannot be established from the data alone.

"It's almost impossible to tell from the data; you have to know who was trading and on what information," Grégoire said, noting that it's reasonable for the institutions involved to investigate whether their processes had been compromised.

A Polymarket spokeswoman told the Times that the company spotted the trades immediately and is investigating. Kalshi declined to comment to the Times.

The Times also reported that spokespeople for the Times and the Siena Research Institute said they took the allegations seriously and planned to review the research. Times spokesperson Charlie Stadtlander said the newspaper prohibits employees from using unpublished coverage to make investments.

Lyle Durbin, CEO of ReconMR, the data collection firm that owns the Siena Research Institute, said employees are bound by confidentiality and nondisclosure agreements prohibiting the use of unpublished data for any purpose, according to the Times.

World Liberty Financial declined to comment through a representative. Kathleen Clark, a spokesperson for Vanderbilt's Institute of National Security, told The Block that Goldstein would not be able to comment. The Block also sought comment from Polymarket, Kalshi, the Times and Siena but has not heard back as of publication time.

The Times' scrutiny comes as prediction markets face broader questions about their level of oversight over their own platforms. Both Kalshi and Polymarket tightened their insider trading rules earlier this year amid scrutiny from the Senate.

In April, the Justice Department charged U.S. Army soldier Gannon Ken Van Dyke with using classified information about the operation to capture former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro to make about $409,881 on Polymarket. A former White House teleprompter operator agreed in August to pay $172,000 to settle CFTC charges that he used advance knowledge of Trump's speeches to bet on Kalshi's "mention markets."

#5108. Binance to Close Accounts in Four EU Countries Dec. 31, Charging Idle Balances From Nov. 29

Binance will close customer accounts in France, Spain, Italy and Poland on Dec. 31 and begin charging a monthly fee on leftover balances from Nov. 29, as the world’s largest crypto exchange still lacks a license under the European Union’s MiCA rules.

A Binance spokesperson confirmed to CoinDesk the closure date over the weekend, saying the exchange is “progressively restricting certain services for affected users” now that the transition period under the Markets in Crypto-Assets framework has ended.

“Affected accounts will be closed with effect from 31 December 2026,” the spokesperson said, calling it “a carefully managed process.” Binance said customer assets remain safe and accessible and described the move as “no sudden freeze.”

Deposits are already blocked, but users can still withdraw crypto and fiat. Users in the four countries have shared the notices on X.

According to emails that users shared, Binance will charge 0.025% of remaining crypto balances each month, or 10 USDC, whichever is higher, starting Nov. 29. The first charge lands within three days of that date, and a second follows after Dec. 24.

Fees come out of USDC first and then other assets. Accounts holding less than the fee would lose the entire balance, the emails say. Anyone who withdraws everything before Nov. 29 pays nothing.

Binance had not published those fee figures in a public announcement as of Sunday, so the details rest on the customer emails, not an official notice. The company’s spokesperson confirmed only the closure date and the restriction of services.

The four countries share one trait: Binance ran locally registered businesses in each before the MiCA transition ended. Its first outreach to users there came in June, when it urged them to withdraw.

MiCA requires crypto firms to win authorization in one EU member state before serving customers across the bloc. Binance withdrew its Greek license application in June and said it would seek approval elsewhere, without naming the country.

Reuters had reported that month that Greece’s Hellenic Capital Market Commission was poised to reject the bid, as Bitcoin Magazine reported. As the MiCA deadline arrived, the EU’s securities regulator also ordered unlicensed crypto firms to exit the market.

Binance told users at the time that it remained committed to Europe. It does not appear on the European Securities and Markets Authority’s register of authorized crypto providers, and it has not said which regulator now holds its application.

The closures arrive in a rough stretch for the exchange. Hackers stole about $6.3 billion from Binance on Oct. 29 in a record heist the company said began with malware hidden in software downloads and fake job offers targeting engineers. Binance has said it will fully reimburse affected customers, and U.S. banking regulators have since quizzed banks following the hack.

On Saturday, Binance also said finance chief Kaiser Ng is leaving after more than five years. Co-CEO Yi He said in a statement that the transition was planned over the last half year, and Justin Yek, most recently a finance vice president at Airwallex, takes over as CFO.

The FBI has blamed a North Korean hacking group for the theft, which Pyongyang has denied.

It is not clear how many Binance users are affected, and there is no sign so far that accounts in Germany or other EU countries are being closed. Notices cited by French media said accounts still holding assets after Dec. 31 would be treated as inactive.

Rivals that already hold MiCA licenses, including Coinbase and Kraken, stand to gain if Binance exits, as noted in June. The test for Binance is whether it can secure a license before more of its European business is wound down.
