#4980. Microsoft-Backed Space and Time Launches Platform to Let AI Agents Verify Private Data

Microsoft-Backed Space and Time Launches Platform to Let AI Agents Verify Private Data

Space and Time, a Microsoft-backed blockchain data project, has launched a platform designed to let businesses use private data in AI tools without handing over the underlying records, the company announced on Monday.

Space and Time Enterprise lets companies prove that calculations made on their private data are correct, so that AI applications, auditors and other stakeholders can check the results without accessing the database itself. Launch partners include onchain asset manager Maple Finance, Indonesian vehicle maker and distributor Indomobil, Oracle software consultancy Trenez and AI data network The Data Foundation.

"Businesses want AI agents to execute workflows using that information, but those agents don't need unrestricted access to the records behind every calculation," Nate Holiday, CEO of MakeInfinite Labs, the original developer of Space and Time, told Decrypt.

The stakes are high, he argued. "The potential cost to a business is the overall value of the business," Holiday said. "Giving AI tools unrestricted access to data creates another avenue of exposure."

Holiday said the company is working with customers in industries including construction, engineering and manufacturing that want to rebuild their systems around AI, so that agents, reporting tools and compliance teams can work from the same business records and check the calculations they rely on.

The platform is built on Proof of SQL, Space and Time's patented technology for proving that database queries were computed correctly. It already works with Google Cloud's BigQuery, and businesses can now run it on their own servers without operating a blockchain, buying specialized hardware or relying on an outside proving service, according to the company.

With GPU acceleration, supported queries over millions of rows can be run and proven in under a second, Holiday said, though actual performance depends on the query, hardware and dataset.

The technology proves that a calculation was carried out correctly on a given set of records, but it cannot tell whether those records were accurate to begin with. "An incorrect entry still needs to be caught through the business's data controls," Holiday said, "but verification gives those controls a much stronger foundation: evidence of which data was used and whether it was processed correctly."

Space and Time raised $20 million in 2022 in a strategic funding round led by M12, Microsoft's venture fund. Last year, Grayscale launched a trust giving investors exposure to SXT, the token that powers Space and Time's blockchain. Earlier this year, the company launched Dreamspace, an AI-powered tool built with Microsoft's Azure AI services that lets users create and deploy onchain apps without writing code.

Space and Time also runs SXT Chain, a decentralized network that supplies verifiable data to DeFi, tokenized assets and stablecoins. Asked whether enterprise customers would create demand for SXT, Holiday said businesses could deploy the product in their own environment, and that the network would come into play as their applications "need to connect to onchain markets," with those workloads paid for through Space and Time.

The company's cryptography is also used elsewhere. Microsoft's Nova project includes Blitzar, Space and Time's cryptographic acceleration library, and the team's work on a commitment scheme called Dory contributed to a16z crypto's Jolt proof system, Holiday said.

#4977. Steve Buscemi’s Secret to Stealing a Scene

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

“My job is to come in and pretend like it’s my movie!”

I’M LOOKING RIGHT into Steve Buscemi’s eyes, which on this early October morning happen to sit behind a pair of black-framed glasses, stylishly matching the collared shirt and jacket he’s wearing. Looking into Buscemi’s eyes is kinda like looking at the sun—even when he isn’t quite as animated as some of the characters he plays, his demeanor comes across warm and thoughtful. The 68-year-old is a New York native, and it’s easy to picture an alternate world where he’s the neighbor who always greets you in the hallway—instead of a face you’ve seen in a number of classic movies and shows that probably approaches triple digits.

Much like the sun, sometimes Buscemi’s eyes—and the rest of him—are at their strongest when doled out in limited quantities, adding an indescribable excellence to a movie or show in need of some extra oomph.

His latest role comes in Wild Horse Nine, a darkly comedic thriller from writer/director Martin McDonagh, the Oscar-winning Irish filmmaker behind movies like In Bruges and The Banshees of Inisherin. The film follows a pair of longtime friends and CIA officers, Chris (John Malkovich) and Lee (Sam Rockwell), in 1973 as they visit Easter Island in Chile, just ahead of the infamous coup d’état. Chris, who is terminally ill, is essentially being put out to pasture by the agency, and wants to take a visit from their station in Santiago, and Lee joins him. Buscemi plays a supporting role, popping up throughout its runtime as MJ, the spectacularly dressed and morally dubious boss to our lead duo.

Buscemi has played lead roles before, including, most notably, in HBO’s hit series Boardwalk Empire, for which he won a Golden Globe and was nominated for two Emmys. But it’s those supporting roles—in everything from blockbusters like Con Air to classics like Reservoir Dogs and The Big Lebowski to cameos in Adam Sandler movies—that have made him iconic, and it’s those kinds of movies where people see him put some of his best work on display. How, then, does he so regularly make a character like MJ feel lived-in and three-dimensional in a movie that mostly follows Chris and Lee?

“I don’t really worry about what their story is,” he explains with a laugh. “You know? My job is to come in and pretend like it’s my movie!”

He explains that he read Frank Capra’s autobiography, The Name Above the Title, where the It’s a Wonderful Life director makes it clear that he considers every character in one of his films to be worthy of their own standalone narrative. “The thing I care about most is the part I’m playing… it doesn’t matter how big it is,” he says. “But are they important to the story? It doesn’t matter how much screen time he has.”

It’s a busy season for Buscemi, who’s not only in Wild Horse Nine, but also plays a key supporting role alongside his old friend (and fellow Coen brothers collaborator) John Turturro in The Only Living Pickpocket in New York. As the former film hits theaters and with the latter already out, Buscemi spoke to Men’s Health about locking in on his roles, close friendships, and memories with the cast of The Big Lebowski.

MEN’S HEALTH: What draws you to a project?

STEVE BUSCEMI: I mean, I like compelling characters. I like ensemble films and ensemble casts. I like good writing and good directing, and with Martin you get that in one package. Knowing it was a Martin McDonagh script, already I’m saying yes before I even read the script, practically. I’m reading it going, [Laughs] I hope I love this as much as I think I’m going to love it. And I did.

MH: Is there any learning curve when you’re working with someone new, who has such a specific vision of what he wants?

SB: There was a bit of nervousness, only because I wanted to do a good job. I wanted to do my best. I did have the advantage of getting to hang out with him in Venice when Banshees of Inisherin was there, four years ago. And we really got on really well. We laughed a lot together, and so that put me at ease, knowing that he was a gentle soul, and seemed like he was going to be very open and collaborative. But I was still just nervous because, you know, the material is just so high up there, and knowing it was going to be John, and Sam… you never want to be the weak link [Laughs]. I just wanted to do my best.

MH: Speaking of John and Sam, you guys have this very lived-in dynamic—did you have to work ahead of time to achieve that?

SB: I’ve known Sam for like 35 years. I don’t always get to see him, but whenever I do, we just kind of fall back to that easiness of, we just really like each other and want to know what we’re up to. We have a lot of mutual friends, and people we’ve worked with. I don’t know John as well, but I have worked with him, and naturally, the scene we did in the office, that’s the only scene I have with John, we filmed that later. So, I got to hang out with him quite a bit when we were in Rapa Nui, on Easter Island. We were all staying at the same resort, so it was very easy to run into each other. And everything about our relationship—not that I have such a big relationship with John—but I’ve known him for a long time, I really respect him, I admire him, and I think I would be very intimidated if I were to direct him. So imagining that I would be his boss [laughs], it’s like, you want his respect, but if he doesn’t give it to you, how crushing that would be? I think that really served my character well.

MH: Is there any sort of mental hurdle to that? Like, locking in and being like ‘I am John Malkovich’s boss.”

SB: You just dive in. The material is so good, and John is so nice. He’s not an actor that will play games with you on set, or try and intimidate you. He’s just so open and easy to work with. I love that, because then you can really feel like ‘Oh, if I go at him hard, it’s OK.’ Or if he goes at me hard, it’s fine. We’re not going to take it personally.

MH: Does shooting on location in Chile come with a different experience?

SB: In Santiago, we were shooting in the square where the coup happened. So, that was very powerful just to imagine that this is where those real events happened. Then shooting on Easter Island, Rapa Nui… that’s a magical place. The horses really do run wild, and the dogs run wild, and there’s chickens and cows, and the Moai are incredible, and the history there. Like I said, we were all staying in the same resort, so it was just all about the work. There weren’t any real distractions. Everywhere you look informed the work that we were doing.

MH: Did you do any kind of research ahead of time?

SB: I asked Martin, “What should I be reading?” and he suggested this one book on the CIA that was very… I don’t know, it got very in the weeds, and the main gist of it—I couldn’t even finish it because it was just so disheartening to know what the CIA had been up to. But there’s a lot of big egos, and maybe that was the main thing I took away from it. I think MJ is very ambitious, and I love the outfits that he wore. That was one thing that really informed me—like, OK, this guy thinks a lot of himself. That’s good. I think I had first envisioned him as more of a bureaucrat in simple suits. So, I like that about the character, and I liked that he had that other dimension to him, where he had that coping mechanism. That, to me, made him very human.

MH: Is it fun to bounce back and forth between genres?

SB: As an actor, I’ve always wanted to sort of be able to do it all, and not be confined to any one genre. When I first started out, I did a lot of independent films, which I loved. It was in the late ’80s and ’90s, and I think that was a time when independent film was really coming into its own. But I never wanted to just be known as, like, “the independent film guy.” I wanted to do other things. I wanted to work in studio movies, comedies, TV—and I feel very lucky that I’ve been able to explore and get work in all these different types of films, and I don’t feel constricted to be any one way or to do any one thing.

MH: Does that inform how you approach and play your latest roles?

SB: I don’t know that it informs how I would do it, but I think, certainly, maybe the work that Martin has seen me do in the past, like with the Coen brothers, or Quentin, or Boardwalk, made him think I was right for this character. Whatever the reason he thought of me, I’m happy [laughs].

MH: Was there any kind of bump in the road along the way on Wild Horse Nine?

SB: Not really a bump in the road, but just wanting to do my best. I have a monologue at the end, where I’m telling Sam’s character the way things are going to go, and the way things are, and I knew that was an important part of the story, and part of MJ’s character. And you just want to get it right. These days, I have to spend more time on the script, and learning the lines, and getting it right, than when I was younger, when I used to just be able to pick things up very quickly. It takes more work now, but it’s OK [laughs].

MH: At the end of the day, Wild Horse Nine is a story of close friendship between these two guys who would do anything for each other. Is there anyone in the industry who’s like that for you?

SB: I have a very old friend, Mark Boone Junior, and we used to write and perform our own theater pieces, and I feel like it was with him that I learned a lot about being an actor. I also learned how to write, because he and I used to write our own shows, and also, by default, we would direct ourselves. So, that was the beginning of me directing. I feel like I owe a lot to him, and that time in my life. We live on different coasts and we don’t see each other a lot, but whenever we do, we just always fall back into that ease of friendship. He’s somebody I would do anything for, or anything that he asked of me.

MH: I have to end with a question about The Big Lebowski, which is my favorite movie of all time. Because not only have I just seen you in Wild Horse Nine, but I just saw John Goodman in Digger, and you’re also in Only Living Pickpocket in New York with John Turturro. What kind of relationship do you all have these days?

SB: I was very happy to work with John Turturro on Pickpocket, because I hadn’t worked with him in years and years. We know each other—I don’t live in Brooklyn anymore, but we used to live in the same neighborhood. I see him around town, or at screenings, or festivals. And so it was so satisfying and gratifying to be able to do a movie with him in New York.

Goodman, I haven’t seen in a while, but a few years ago, I don’t know what anniversary it was of The Big Lebowski, but me, John, and Jeff Bridges sat down for an interview, and then we had dinner afterwards, and it was just… I don’t even think we hung out that much during the making of the movie. But that dinner, that night, we just had the best time. And we were looking at each other, going, ‘Why didn’t we do this while we were making the movie?’ [Laughs] But that’s the nature of it—you know, we were filming in L.A., and when you’re filming in your home town, I mean, I don’t live there, but it’s such a spread out town. You do the work, and then you go off and do other things. That’s what I loved about shooting in Rapa Nui—we were just all there, and so we not only had the great memory of making the movie, but also hanging out together and having dinners, and seeing each other at breakfast.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

#4976. The $2.85B Binance hackers lost shows why the UK needs stablecoin freeze rules

The Binance hack proved stablecoin freezes work. Now the UK should make clear freeze rules a condition of listing coins.

Opinion by Lissele Pratt, co-founder, Capitalixe

Last weekend, the Federal Bureau of Investigation claimed that North Korea was behind the $6.3 billion Binance hack, which analysts are calling the largest heist on record.

While I’ve read a lot about the scale of this heist, far less has been said about what went right.

Because while it is true that the perpetrators stole $6.3 billion, they haven’t actually been able to hold on to a significant proportion of those takings. $2.85 billion of the funds has been frozen.

The reason for this comes down to what the stolen money was comprised of. Of that $2.85 billion, 94% was held in stablecoins.

While this hack was terrible news all around, it did show that centralized control, oftentimes criticized by crypto purists, might actually be one of the strongest tools we have against crypto crime.

Because, unlike other assets, stablecoins can be frozen by their issuers, giving victims a real chance to stop stolen money reaching the wrong hands.

It does, however, raise an uncomfortable question. If private companies have the power to freeze billions of dollars in assets, who decides when they should be allowed to use it?

The United Kingdom now has the opportunity to do just that.

To get an idea of just how much the freeze mattered, you just have to look at what the hackers did within the first hour of their heist.

Related: Binance, Polymarket seek Polygon hard fork to recover $6.8M in stolen crypto

Clearly, they understood that there was a very limited window to move the stablecoins before anyone could stop them. So that’s exactly what they did.

What was frozen from the Binance hack. Source: Elliptic

Less than an hour after the hack began, they had already sold $325 million worth of stolen USDT (Tether’s stablecoin) for Ether. The reasoning behind this was to swap an asset that could be frozen with one that could not.

Luckily, stablecoin issuers were quick to act, with Tether’s first major freeze coming just 37 minutes after the stolen funds reached the hackers’ main wallet, and Circle following three minutes later, blocking the movement of USDC.

Without Tether and Circle intervening, far more of the funds could have ended up beyond their reach.

The freeze, to its credit, worked for the most part because a handful of companies decided, in real time, to use it.

The problem is that there still isn’t an agreed standard that outlines when a freeze is justified or how quickly the response needs to be, and we’re beginning to see the consequences of not having these answers.

For instance, Circle is facing a proposed class action from Drift Protocol investors who allege it failed to freeze around $230 million in stolen USDC after the April hack. Their argument is that Circle could have stopped the money from moving, but didn’t act in time.

Meanwhile, Tether is being sued in New York by two businessmen who claim it froze millions of their assets months before a seizure warrant existed. It’s important to mention that Tether has responded to this case, calling these allegations baseless.

Working in payments and banking, it’s not uncommon for me to see traditional banks freeze money and accounts. Due to this, you may argue that if a bank can do this frequently, why shouldn’t a stablecoin issuer be able to?

Stablecoin freeze timeline. Source: CertiK

My argument isn’t that they shouldn’t. But banks don’t get to decide they want to freeze assets out of nowhere. There’s usually a legal trigger behind their decision, as well as a way to challenge it.

Stablecoin issuers now hold the same power while having to act faster and across borders. So it’s about time they’re given some structure.

There is, however, a caveat with regulating stablecoin issuers. Tether and Circle, two of the largest issuers, aren’t based in the UK, so British regulators can’t simply tell them what to do.

Related: Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

What Britain can control, though, is which stablecoins UK customers are allowed to buy, and they’ve already begun doing so.

In June, the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) published its cryptoassets regime. Under it, UK trading platforms must carry out due diligence and publish a disclosure document before they list a coin, and that includes stablecoins issued overseas. It comes into force on 25 October 2027.

The Bank of England is finalizing its Code of Practice for systemic stablecoin issuers by the end of this year. It covers stablecoins used widely enough for payments to matter to UK financial stability.

Neither framework establishes the clear obligations around freezing stolen funds that I believe are necessary. But both are still taking shape, giving the UK the perfect opportunity to address this gap now, rather than trying to retrofit new requirements later.

So here’s a solution that I believe would work. Before a stablecoin can be sold on a UK platform, its issuer should have to agree to two conditions.

The first being that it is contactable and ready to act. Tether and Circle did exactly that last week, but the victim was the world’s biggest exchange. A small UK platform should get the same response.

I work with a number of smaller crypto businesses that don’t have Binance’s profile or a direct line to Tether. Under the current system, they’re the ones most likely to be left waiting.

The second is that there needs to be a clear timetable for what happens next. If an issuer freezes funds, it should notify the National Crime Agency that same day. The freeze should then expire if there hasn’t been a court order put out to keep the wallet frozen.

Neither of these are extremely controversial takes. They’d give law enforcement a chance to intervene when money is stolen and also protect people from having their assets frozen indefinitely, without legal grounds.

The Binance hack has shown us that stablecoins can give victims of crypto crime a chance to stop stolen money before it’s gone for good. That shouldn’t be a privilege reserved only for the world’s biggest exchanges.

#4972. Build Muscle After 40 With This Full-Body Workout Plan

This 12-week program can help you get the gains you want as an older guy.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

AS YOU GET older, the way you approach life should change. You have more wisdom than you did when you were a young man from all your years of experience—or at least, one hopes, you should—but in some settings, like the gym, you'll need to adjust your behavior even more. You might not realize it, but your body changes when you age.

That's why men over 40 should take a slightly different approach to fitness than their younger peers. You can probably still hang with younger guys—but that doesn’t mean you always should. You’ll be even stronger if you shift your workout plan to an emphasis on careful, calculated programming.

The Men's Health Muscle After 40 book gives you that smart, measured plan you need for your workout as an older man.

The 12-week fitness guide doesn't give you handicapped workouts with instructions to play it safe—the plan is designed to bring out the best in you while allowing you to rest and recover within your body's capacity. Muscle After 40 is split into 3 distinct phases, each of which consist of 4 weeks where you'll train 3 times.

IF YOU KNOW what works best for you, keep doing it. That said, if what you do is walk into the weight room and start lifting with no warmup at all, I predict it won’t work for much longer. The older you get, and the more miles you put on the chassis, the trickier it is to get your body ready for a good workout.

Your warmup should at least have these two components:

THIS PROGRAM REQUIRES basic equipment you’ll find in most commercial gyms. Don’t worry if your gym doesn’t have everything on the list. It’s easy to substitute similar exercises.

IN EACH PHASE of this workout plan, you’ll train three times a week for four weeks. Most will use the classic Monday-Wednesday-Friday or Tuesday-Thursday-Saturday schedule. Remember to include at least one full day of rest between workouts. You’ll do each exercise as straight sets, completing all the sets of one exercise before moving onto the next.

FOR THE FIRST exercise on Day 1 (dumbbell incline bench press) and Day 2 (trap-bar deadlift) you’ll use the sets across method, which means you’ll use the same weight for all your work sets. (It’s the opposite of ramping sets, in which you use a heavier weight on each set, building up to a final set with a max weight.) With the sets across method, you don’t increase the weight until you hit all the reps on every set.

Let’s say you’re doing 3 sets of 6 to 8 reps. After doing a warmup set with a lighter weight, you select a weight you figure you can lift 10 times. You easily complete 8 reps the first set, and could’ve done at least two or three more. On the second set, you also get 8 reps, although this time you only have one or two left in the tank. On the third set, you can only complete 7.

The next week, you use that same weight for all your sets, with the goal of getting 8 good reps on each one. More important, you feel like you could’ve completed at least one or two more on every set. That means you’re cleared to increase the weight by 2.5 to5 percent for the following week.

With the rest of the exercises in Phase 1 that are not indicated as using the sets across method, you can use more weight on subsequent sets if your first-set weight was too light and you easily completed all the reps. Increase the weight when you exceed the range by 2 or 3 reps on every set. So if the workout calls for 2 sets of 12 to 15 reps, and you hit 17 or 18 on the final set, use a heavier weight the next time you do that exercise.

IN PHASE 1 and Phase 2, I don’t specify how to long to rest between sets or exercises. Here area couple of guidelines to follow:

YOU'LL SEE SOME progressions in volume within the workouts, specifically the number of sets. With some exercises, you’ll increase the number of sets (from 2 to 3) after Week 1. Then, in Week 4, you’ll decrease volume across the board, doing 2 sets of every exercise to give your muscles a break before starting Phase 2.

Want the full program? Check out Muscle After 40 before you get started with Phase 1.

#4971. Analysts see no broader ‘contagion’ from $6.3B Binance hack

Binance’s pledge to cover all user losses helped contain the fallout from the $6.3 billion hack, analysts said.

Binance’s pledge to cover user losses from its $6.3 billion hack helped contain the fallout across crypto markets, analysts told Cointelegraph.

“There doesn’t seem to be any contagion-type effect from the hack, given that Binance is able to cover the losses using its balance sheet,” Martin Lee, market insights lead at DWF Labs, told Cointelegraph. He said investors turned their focus to the implications of the US midterm elections.

Binance’s daily net outflows fell to $458 million in the 24 hours leading to Monday’s publication time, down from $6.1 billion on Nov. 2 after withdrawals resumed, according to DefiLlama.

Attackers stole about $6.3 billion from Binance hot wallets in the Oct. 29 breach, making it the largest crypto hack to date. Bitcoin (BTC) fell as much as 5.7% over the first five days after the hack, a decline CryptoQuant called “modest” in its Wednesday report .

Bitcoin fell roughly 13.6% after the $1.4 billion Bybit hack in 2025 and about 9.9% after the Coincheck hack in 2018. At least 14 of the past 38 hacks CryptoQuant reviewed were followed by steeper Bitcoin declines.

Bitcoin’s drawdown after the Binance hack, compared to previous exchange hacks. Source: CryptoQuant

Major exchange hacks have previously triggered “short-lived drawdowns that markets later absorbed” as long-term holder demand returned, Lacie Zhang, research lead at Bitget Wallet, told Cointelegraph, adding that the Binance hack does not represent a “structural trend reversal” for crypto markets.

Related: Binance hackers had 8-week intrusion window before $6.3B exploit: SEAL

“Much of the hack’s impact now looks priced in,” with Bitcoin currently trading at an “attractive level,” Bitfinex analysts told Cointelegraph.

“Confirmation of a market bottom now depends on the Consumer Price Index (CPI) reading due on 10 November, which will shape the Federal Reserve’s next rate decision. Higher bond yields would put pressure on risk assets.”

Market participants priced in an 80% chance that the Federal Reserve will raise rates at its Dec. 9 meeting, up from 69% a month earlier, according to the CMEGroup’s FedWatch tool.

Bitcoin fell 2.5% over 24 hours to $74,580 as of 1:21 pm UTC on Monday, according to CoinMarketCap.

#4970. NYSE Taps Avalanche to Settle Its Planned Tokenized Stock Platform

NYSE Taps Avalanche to Settle Its Planned Tokenized Stock Platform

The New York Stock Exchange is turning to Avalanche to power the behind-the-scenes plumbing of its forthcoming tokenized securities platform, one of the clearest signs yet that Wall Street’s oldest institutions are moving core market infrastructure onto public blockchains.

Ava Labs, the company behind Avalanche, said Monday that the blockchain will serve as the “core ledger technology” for the clearing firm behind an NYSE-affiliated digital trading venue.

The platform, which the NYSE’s parent Intercontinental Exchange previously said it was developing, is designed to allow 24/7 trading, clearing and settlement of tokenized securities, subject to regulatory approval. Running on Avalanche, it aims to bring the clearing of tokenized equities onchain at institutional scale.

Tokenization, the practice of issuing traditional assets as blockchain-based tokens, has become one of Wall Street’s buzziest pursuits, but most efforts so far have centered on individual products rather than the market machinery itself.

The NYSE’s move is notable because it puts a blockchain underneath the clearing and settlement layer, the plumbing that finalizes trades, rather than just wrapping a single fund or stock in a token.

Dennis Lee, the NYSE’s head of digital issuance, said the exchange is “picking the right technology for each job,” and that while its planned platform will support equities listed across a range of blockchains, the ledger recording and settling trades needs to be fast, reliable and able to control who participates.

Emin Gün Sirer, Ava Labs’ CEO and founder, framed the deal as proof that blockchains can support not just products but the infrastructure they trade on, citing Avalanche’s “24/7 atomic settlement” and predictable costs.

The announcement adds to a frenetic race to put stocks and their underlying infrastructure onchain.

The NYSE separately tapped Blockchain.com to reach crypto investors with tokenized stocks and ETFs, while ICE and crypto exchange OKX are planning a 24/7 tokenized stock venue under an SEC exemption, and Morgan Stanley has moved to tokenize securities.

That OKX-ICE effort has hit friction, though: Every Magnificent Seven stock, including Apple and Meta, opted out of having its shares tokenized on the venue, with 67 companies in all joining them.

Research shows the appetite for tokenized stocks is real but uneven. An IMF study found tokenized stocks show genuine demand yet remain volatile and illiquid, a reminder that the infrastructure may be arriving faster than the markets to fill it.

#4967. Tom Lee’s Bitmine Adds $20M in Ethereum, Seizing on Vitalik’s 'ETH Is Money' Declaration

Tom Lee’s Bitmine Adds $20M in Ethereum, Seizing on Vitalik’s 'ETH Is Money' Declaration

Bitmine Immersion Technologies extended its weekly Ethereum buying streak, adding 9,587 ETH worth roughly $20 million, as chairman Tom Lee pointed to a recent declaration by Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin as validation of the firm’s accumulation strategy.

The NYSE-listed company said Monday its holdings reached 6,070,092 ETH, worth roughly $13.6 billion at a reference price of $2,234 per coin.

That’s 4.97% of Ethereum’s 122.2 million token supply, leaving Bitmine, by its own count, 99% of the way to its goal of owning 5% of the network, a target it calls the “Alchemy of 5%,” reached in just 15 months.

The firm has bought Ethereum every week since launching its treasury strategy on June 30, 2025.

Lee leaned heavily on comments Buterin made at the Devcon conference in Mumbai, where the Ethereum founder said, “I’ve finally made up my mind, ETH is a store of value, ETH is money.” Lee called the declaration “a seismic shift,” arguing it clarifies how ETH will capture value in what he described as a coming “supercycle of tokenization and AI.” (Disclosure: Tom Lee is one of several investors in Dastan, Decrypt’s parent company.)

He contended ETH functions as “pristine and trusted collateral” rather than a cash-flow token, reflecting its position as the layer-1 network favored by financial institutions moving assets onchain.

Buterin elaborated in an X post, laying out why he now sees ETH primarily as a store of value, including his view that fee-burning is weaker than it once seemed and that optimizing for it can conflict with decentralization. Lee framed that reasoning as consistent with Ethereum’s roadmap and its role in the tokenization wave.

Counting cash, other tokens and “moonshot” investments, Bitmine’s total holdings stood at $14.3 billion as of Nov. 8, including 216 Bitcoin, $475 million in cash and marketable securities, a $180 million stake in Beast Industries and a $75 million position in Eightco Holdings, which it bills as a rare public proxy for indirect OpenAI exposure.

Bitmine remains the world’s largest Ethereum treasury and the second-largest crypto treasury overall, trailing only Michael Saylor’s Strategy, which the release said holds about 849,327 BTC. Bitmine has 5,227,037 ETH, roughly 86% of its holdings, staked through its MAVAN platform, with projected annualized staking revenue of about $359 million.

The firm also recently argued the muted market reaction to the record Binance hack showed crypto’s growing resilience.

#4963. A Guy’s Guide to the Many (Many) Dick Myths of 2026

A urologist right-sizes the manhood myths that have recently sprung up online.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

From biohacker Bryan Johnson to looksmaxxer Clavicular, every influencer claims to have the secret to looking bigger, getting harder, and lasting longer. “Penis-maxxing can run the gamut,” says Rena Malik, MD, a urologist, sexual health expert, and Men's Health advisor. “It's a huge market.” (Pun probably intended.) Do any of their tactics work though? The jury is out, but the doctor is in: “I want people to think about the long-term health of their penis, not just right now,” Dr. Malik says, which is why she wrote a whole book about it (The Hard Truth, out now). Here, she right-sizes the manhood myths that have recently sprung up online.

Sometimes (note: not “often”), the ingredients in these supplements do work for some men (note: not “all”—or “many”). That could be because of L-arginine and L-citrulline, ingredients that increase nitric oxide and allow more blood to flow to the penis, says Dr. Malik. Or it could also be the placebo effect.

The supplement industry is largely unregulated and many products have little to no scientific backing. Some supplements even contain contraband PDE5 inhibitors, like tadalafil and sildenafil, or animal testosterone. Which means they might work, but they can also interact with other meds or come with side effects, Dr. Malik says. Plus, the time you waste trying different OTC options can lead to (gulp) shrinkage as erectile tissue becomes fibrotic. So, if you think you have ED, skip the supplements and see your doc, like, now.

The theory behind jelqing, which is giving yourself an extra intense hand job, is that doing so creates micro-tears. In muscle-building, at least, the healing of micro-tears can result in strength and size overall. But…

…your penis isn’t a muscle. Which means that jelqing could cause serious irreversible damage to the blood vessels in your penis and to your penis itself. The so called “technique” has no science behind it and can even cause permanent ED, Dr. Malik says. You’re better off working out your pelvic floor.

Some research does link ejaculation to certain health improvements (less stress, deeper sleep, even immune function).

A lot of guys think they need to have sex or masturbate whenever they get hard to keep their erectile tissue healthy. “You need to keep getting erections to keep your tissue healthy, and your body does that as long as you're healthy,” says Dr. Malik. If you regularly notice that you’re not hard when you wake up in the middle of the night, or you don’t have morning wood, see your doc. It may be a sign of fibrosis of the erectile tissue, which can lead to penis shrinkage.

In specialized cases, a penis pump or medical-grade vacuum erection device (VED) can help some men. Both devices create negative pressure to draw blood into the penis, which can result in an erection. For guys about to get a penile implant, a penis pump can help them “get a little bit of a larger implant,” says Dr. Malik. For men who have had a radical prostatectomy (that’s a surgery for prostate cancer that can lead to a loss of penile length), they can maintain their penile length with VED.

There's little to no data that penis pumps work to enhance length or girth for the average guy, Dr. Malik says. In fact, the European Association of Urology reviewed all the science on penile enhancement with VEDs and found scarce evidence that these devices provide any aesthetic benefit. What does: Manscaping. An overgrown bush can hide a large part of your penis, so grooming your pubic hair is easy (and way cheaper).

Testosterone levels do start to dip after age 40. And T factors into a strong libido, erectile function, and sexual satisfaction. For men who develop testosterone deficiency and experience symptoms (low desire, ED, etc.), TRT can help.

Testosterone levels fluctuate all the time—"the variability even within individuals ranges from 18 to 50 percent,” says Dr. Malik. So, if you haven’t been into sex, or can’t get it up once in a while, your T may not be the issue. First focus on eating healthier, reducing stress, and sleeping better. If the issues stick around, then see a doctor.

Honestly, there isn’t one. Your penis is fine.

Being bigger won’t necessarily make you better in bed. That’s because 85 percent of women need clitoral stimulation to orgasm, Dr. Malik says, which typically doesn’t happen through vaginal penetration alone. Because the clitoris is not located at the back of the vaginal canal (that’s the cervix, and hitting it can be painful for some women), a longer penis doesn’t mean more pleasure. A skilled tongue, faster fingers, and/or the right tools (read: a vibrator) will though.

Lindsay Geller is the Lifestyle Director at Women’s Health, where she oversees the Life, Sex & Love, and Relationships sections on WomensHealthMag.com and the Mind section of Women's Health magazine. When she's not writing or editing articles about the latest dating trends and pop culture phenomenons, she's usually watching reality TV or playing with her dog, Lucille (Go Fetch That) Ball.

#4961. Avalanche’s AVAX leads crypto gains following NYSE tokenization deal

Avalanche’s AVAX token rose as much as 10% after the NYSE selected its blockchain to support settlement on a planned tokenized stock trading platform.

Avalanche’s AVAX token jumped as much as 10% on Monday, making it the top earner among the 100 largest cryptocurrencies by market capitalization, as the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) announced plans to use its blockchain to settle trades on a new platform for trading tokenized stocks around the clock.

AVAX later eased to $8.86, still up over 8% on the day, according to CoinGecko data as of 03:22 p.m. UTC. The token currently has a market cap of around $3.95 billion, with $439.7 million traded over the past day. The Avalanche blockchain boasts a total value locked (TVL) of $633 million, up 4% over the past 24 hours, according to DeFiLlama.

“At the NYSE, we’re looking at how blockchain technology can expand what we offer in equities, and we’re picking the right technology for each job,” Dennis Lee, the head of digital issuance at NYSE, said in an announcement.

The deal underscores a growing effort by Wall Street firms to use blockchain technology to make stock trading faster and allow investors to trade outside regular market hours. Typically, traditional US stock markets are open from 9:30 am to 4:00 pm EST on weekdays, with trades often taking at least one business day to settle.

The NYSE first announced plans for the platform in January. In September, Ava Labs revealed that the exchange had spent about a year testing Avalanche, but the blockchain’s final pick was not confirmed until Monday.

Related: Aave launches V4 on Avalanche, laying groundwork for tokenized credit markets

Ava Labs said that the NYSE will use Avalanche’s blockchain to keep track of stock trades and help complete transactions on its planned trading platform. The platform will let investors buy and sell digital versions of stocks on different blockchains, while Avalanche will be used to record those trades and make sure transactions are completed.

“The upcoming digital trading platform will support equities listed on a range of blockchains, though the ledger that records and settles trades needs to be fast and reliable, let us control who can take part, keep costs predictable, and have a large community of developers behind it. Avalanche meets all those needs,” Lee said.

The platform could launch before the end of 2026 if it receives regulatory approval, according to The Wall Street Journal.

“Our first conversations with the NYSE team began nearly a year ago, and it’s remarkable how quickly we’ve progressed toward supporting this infrastructure,” John Nahas, chief business officer at Ava Labs told Cointelegraph. “It’s an important step toward blockchain becoming foundational financial infrastructure.”

The news comes as other major financial firms are also investing in blockchain technology to make stock trading faster and available around the clock.

In September, Nasdaq announced plans to invest $100 million in Kraken’s parent company, Payward, to launch tokenized stocks. The expected launch for that project is the second quarter of 2027.

Meanwhile, the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation (DTCC), which helps settle US securities trades, launched a service in October that allows financial firms to turn traditional securities into digital tokens.

“Today marks the culmination of years of work to bring tokenized securities into a regulated framework supported by trusted market infrastructure,” Frank La Salla, CEO of DTCC, said at the time. “By extending DTC’s resiliency, governance and operational standards into digital markets, we are establishing the foundation for responsible institutional adoption at scale.”

The NYSE deal also follows other financial firms’ efforts to bring traditional financial assets specifically onto Avalanche. In July, Japanese financial technology company Progmat announced it had moved more than 452 billion yen worth of digital securities onto a dedicated Avalanche blockchain.

And in September, Aave Labs announced plans to launch a lending service on Avalanche that would let financial firms borrow Tether’s USAT stablecoin using tokenized assets as collateral.
